This is an extremely large and complex security audit request. Let me carefully analyze the diff and the surrounding code to find real faults. The prompt is very specific about what to look for, so let me work through each target systematically.

Let me focus on the actual diff (045fcc0..HEAD) and the specific questions asked.

Let me start by understanding what changed:

1. **R1 - The Commitment Scheme**: New `commit_seed` + modified `commit_deck` in the mint program
2. **R2 - `blockhash_of_slot`**: Hand-written raw parse of SlotHashes sysvar
3. **R3 - The halt fix in distribute.mjs**: Tombstone detection for burned vaults


4. **R4 - Two defects already found**: Ordering fix and probe ceiling fix
5. **R5 - Suite new arms**: Section 2.0 and 11.11
6. **R6 - Accepted limits rewrites**: 1a and 23
7. **R7 - Counts and arithmetic**: Floor verification
8. **R8 - One-of-N-sites**: Applied everywhere?

Now I'm diving into the actual code to identify the real issues. Starting with R1, I need to examine the commitment scheme logic in `commit_seed` to see how the salt hash is being handled and whether there are any edge cases or vulnerabilities in the implementation.

The key concern here is whether the Bank can manipulate the entropy slot by repeatedly submitting `commit_seed` until landing in a favorable slot. Since the Bank controls when it submits the transaction, it can influence which slot the commitment lands in, and the entropy slot is calculated as current_slot plus 32 slots ahead. The Bank could theoretically grind through multiple submissions to find a slot whose blockhash produces a favorable seed for the deck.

However, there's a critical constraint: `commit_seed` is one-shot, so the Bank can't re-commit once it's already committed. The real question is whether the Bank can abandon a bad draw entirely by re-initializing the config, but looking at the code, `commit_seed` only writes to the config without resetting `deck_root`, so there's no mechanism to clear a previous commitment and start fresh.

The actual attack vector is that the Bank can choose *when* to submit `commit_seed`, which determines which slot gets chosen (current + 32), and since it knows the salt, it can precompute the deck for any candidate blockhash and wait for a favorable one. The mitigation is that the Bank can't predict the blockhash 32 slots in advance, but it can observe the current blockhash and estimate what the future one might be, then submit strategically.

However, there's a critical constraint: `commit_seed` is one-shot, so once the Bank commits to a salt_hash, it's locked in. The salt itself is fixed before the slot even exists, so the Bank can't change it later. The real question is whether the Bank can grind through multiple salt choices to find one that produces a favorable deck, but since the salt_hash is committed first and the blockhash is determined by the slot timing, the Bank would need to predict the blockhash 32 slots ahead—which is extremely difficult on Solana.

The actual attack vector is more subtle: the Bank could generate many different salts, compute what deck each would produce for a given blockhash, and then choose which salt to commit based on which one yields the best outcome. But this requires knowing the blockhash in advance, which the Bank can't do with certainty. The real constraint is that the Bank has to commit the salt_hash before it knows what blockhash will be used, so it can't selectively choose a salt that produces a favorable deck. The Bank can't actually predict the blockhash 32 slots ahead with certainty, so the grinding attack is limited to probabilistic manipulation rather than guaranteed control. But there's a more pressing issue: the `commit_seed` function doesn't check if the program is paused, so the Bank could commit a seed even when operations are halted, which seems like a gap in the safety checks.

Looking at the `commit_deck` function, it also lacks a pause check, though the actual minting is protected downstream. The real concern is that the Bank could commit a deck root that doesn't match the seed it committed, and while the program doesn't verify this relationship, the seed itself is public so anyone can verify it off-chain. The Bank could still commit a mismatched root and deal cards from a different deck, but this would be detectable.

Now I'm examining the `blockhash_of_slot` function to understand how it reads the SlotHashes sysvar and validates the slot data.

Parsing the sysvar layout...

The function iterates through the entries to find the matching slot, then extracts and validates the hash. The layout appears correct with little-endian u64 values and 40-byte entries, and the bounds checking looks solid. I'm noticing the code doesn't explicitly handle the case where a slot appears twice in the data, though the Solana runtime shouldn't produce duplicates anyway—it would just return the first match. The real concern is the 512-slot window: if `commit_deck` doesn't land within that ~3.4 minute window after `commit_seed`, the entropy slot ages out and minting becomes permanently blocked, which is a serious failure mode.

The code comment acknowledges this is intentional to prevent re-seeding, but it creates a permanent failure state if the window is missed. There's no recovery mechanism—no way to clear the salt hash and start fresh. The `commit_deck` function does check that the salt hash is non-zero, so it won't proceed without a seed, but once `commit_seed` is called, the system is locked into that commitment with no escape hatch if the timing fails.

Now I'm looking at the `distribute.mjs` changes to understand how the tombstone detection works...

The code checks if a burned vault's account data indicates it's no longer a live Core asset by examining the first byte—if it's absent, too short, or doesn't equal 1, it's marked as burned. The catch block defaults to `tomb = false`, which means RPC failures halt the distribution rather than silently skipping vaults, and this is the right behavior since you can't assume a vault is burned just because you can't read it.

Now I'm checking what other account states could have `data[0] !== 1` besides `Key::Uninitialized` (0) — there's `Key::AssetV1` (1) which is the live state we're looking for, and potentially other variants like `Key::CollectionV1` (2) that would also fail this check.

The real issue is that if `getAccountInfo` returns null, the code treats it as burned, but that could also mean the account was never created or the RPC failed — though the outer try/catch should handle actual RPC errors, so null genuinely indicates the account doesn't exist on chain.

Now I'm looking at the ordering fix in `commit_deck` to see if the one-shot check was moved to the right position...

Checking the ordering of validations...

The `deck_root` check comes first, which is correct since it's the one-shot guard. Then `seedNotCommitted` validates the seed exists before we need it. The salt hash verification happens before the sysvar read, which makes sense since we need the committed value to compare against. The sysvar read and seed derivation follow, and finally the state mutation happens last. This ordering looks sound—no check depends on state that a later check establishes.

Now I'm looking at the probe ceiling fix in the test file...

The probe ceiling was raised to 64, and the assertion checks that `maxAttrs` is between 0 and 63, which means the packet bound was actually reached rather than hitting the ceiling. This can genuinely fail if the packet bound exceeds 64 attributes, though that's unlikely given the 1232-byte limit. The deeper assertion that `maxAttrs` must be less than `MAX_ATTRS_BORSH - 1` (which is 7) is the real constraint—if the packet holds more than 6 attributes, this fails, but that's also unlikely given the size limits.
